Non-conservation of a generalized helicity in the Euler equations
Vikram Giri, Hyunju Kwon, Matthew Novack

Abstract
For a C t , x 1 C^{1}_{t,x} solution u u to the incompressible 3D Euler equations, the helicity H ⁡ ( u ⁡ ( t ) ) = ∫ 𝕋 3 u ⋅ curl ​ u H(u(t))=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}u\cdot\mathrm{curl\,}u is constant in time. For general low-regularity weak solutions, it is not always clear how to define the helicity, or whether it must be constant in time in the case that there is a clear definition. In this paper, we define a generalized helicity which extends the classical definitions and construct weak solutions of Euler of almost Onsager-critical regularity in L 3 L^{3} with prescribed generalized helicity and kinetic energy.



1
Introduction

Given a smooth vector field
v:\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
, its
helicity

H(v)
is defined by

H(v):=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}v(x)\cdot\mathrm{curl\,}v(x)\,dx\,.

(1.1)

Note that the sign of the helicity flips upon reflection of the vector field in a plane; thus the helicity can be regarded as a measure of reflection non-invariance of a vector field. Moreau
[
42
]
and Moffatt
[
41
]
noticed that helicity is a conserved quantity for the three-dimensional incompressible Euler equations

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u+\mathrm{div}(u\otimes u)+\nabla p=0\\
\mathrm{div}\,u=0\end{cases}

(1.2)

posed on
\mathbb{T}^{3}=\mathbb{R}^{3}/\mathbb{Z}^{3}
.
That is, for a smooth solution
(u,p)
of (
1.2
), we have for all
0\leq t\leq T
that

H(u(t))=H(u(0))\,.

(1.3)

1.1 Motivation and background

The helicity of a vector field has a topological interpretation. To see this, let the vorticity
\omega=\mathrm{curl\,}u
be composed of two simple, linked vortex filaments of strengths
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
. More precisely, there exist two smooth, simple, null-homologous, unknotted closed curves
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
that are linked,
\omega
is a measure supported on the
\gamma_{i}
’s with mass
\kappa_{i}
on
\gamma_{i}
, and
\omega(x)
is the unit vector tangent to
\gamma_{i}
at
x
. Let
\Sigma_{i}
be a surface such that
\partial\Sigma_{i}=\gamma_{i}
. Following Moffat
[
41
]
, we can compute the helicity as

\displaystyle H(u)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}u\cdot\mathrm{curl\,}u=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int_{\gamma_{i}}u\cdot\dot{\gamma}_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int_{\Sigma_{i}}\mathrm{curl\,}u=\pm\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}\,\times\,\textnormal{linking number}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\,.

The
\pm
arises due to the two possible relative orientations of the filaments. Thus the helicity of a vector field is related to a topological linking property of the vortex lines. This point of view was clarified by Arnold
[
1
]
, who showed that the helicity of a smooth vector field is equal to an averaged, asymtotic linking number of trajectories of its vorticity.

Furthermore, the helicity obeys a stronger invariance unrelated to the Euler equations: namely, for any volume-preserving diffeomorphism
\Phi
and exact vector field
\omega
, the push-forward vector field
\Phi_{*}\omega=(\nabla\Phi)^{-1}\omega\circ\Phi
satisfies

H(\mathrm{curl\,}^{-1}\Phi_{*}\omega)=H(\mathrm{curl\,}^{-1}\omega)\,.

(1.4)

Note that the push-forward of an exact vector field is exact, and so the
\mathrm{curl\,}^{-1}
in the above equation makes sense. This shows that the helicity is an integral invariant of exact vector fields
\omega
under volume-preserving diffeomorphisms; in fact it is the only such invariant
[
2
,
36
,
25
,
44
,
43
]
. The helicity conservation for smooth solutions of the Euler equations (
1.3
) follows easily from this more general invariance (
1.4
): indeed, the vorticity equation is precisely the statement that the vorticity evolves as the push-forward of the volume-preserving flow of
u
. An extension of helicity to
C^{0}
Hamiltonian structures was recently obtained in
[
26
]
where the authors prove a version of the invariance (
1.4
) for volume-preserving
homeomorphisms
, answering a question of Arnold, in a special case.

However, in turbulent flows exhibiting anomalous dissipation of kinetic energy, the regularity of the vector field
u
is insufficient for the theory of the transport equation to give us a well-behaved, volume-preserving flow. Regularizing the equation, for example by considering a vanishing viscocity limit of Navier-Stokes solutions, might not help due to the problem of vortex reconnections. Therefore in the low regularity setting, the topological interpretation of helicity could break down, and one is left with the analytic definition (
1.1
).

Since there is no global existence theory for smooth solutions of (
1.2
), we must consider weak solutions, which may be square-integrable vector fields but no better. For such weak solutions, however, it is not clear how to interpret (
1.1
), much less check that it is conserved; indeed it would appear that (
1.1
) is only well-defined for vector fields in
\dot{H}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
via the duality pairing
H(v):=\langle v,\mathrm{curl\,}v\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},\dot{H}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}
. While it is known that every weak solution
u
of (
1.2
) which belongs to
L^{3}_{t}B^{\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}_{3,c(\mathbb{N})}\cap L^{\infty}_{t}H^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
does indeed conserve the helicity
[
16
]
– see also
[
14
,
23
,
46
,
32
,
3
]
– the following question has remained open.

Question:
Do there exist weak solutions
u
of (
1.2
) for which the helicity (
1.1
) admits a canonical definition, but for which
H(u(t))
is non-constant in time?

We will answer this question positively using a notion of generalized helicity introduced below. We remark that a stronger version of this question was posed by Buckmaster and Vicol
[
9
, Conjecture 2.7]
, in which the weak solution is required to belong to
C^{0}_{t}H^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
. Our extension of the definition of helicity to rougher vector fields offers an alternative approach.

1.2 Generalized helicity and main result

To define the generalized helicity, we set some conventions regarding Fourier series. We identify a smooth function
f:\mathbb{T}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
with its smooth, periodic lift: the function which satisfies
f(x+k)=f(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
. For
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, the Fourier coefficient
\mathcal{F}(f)(k)
is defined by
\mathcal{F}(f)(k)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}f(x)e^{-2\pi ik\cdot x}
. Note that for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, this definition makes sense on
H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
. Now let
\varphi:\mathbb{Z}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be given and
f\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
; we set

\mathbb{P}_{\varphi}(f)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}\varphi(k)\mathcal{F}(f)(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot x}\,.

(1.5)

A specific example of the above is the
m^{\rm th}
Littlewood-Paley projection
\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}}(f)
of
f
, where
m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and
\varphi_{2^{m}}
is defined as in Definition
1.1
. Note that
\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}}f
is always a smooth function and depends on the choice of base kernel
\varphi
.

Let
u_{1},u_{2}:\mathbb{T}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
be smooth vector fields. We define

\displaystyle H(u_{1},u_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}u_{1}(x)\cdot\mathrm{curl\,}u_{2}(x)\,dx=\sum_{\vec{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}\mathcal{F}(u_{1})(k)\cdot\left(2\pi ik\times\mathcal{F}(u_{2})(-k)\right)\,,

(1.6)

where the latter equality holds due to Parseval’s identity and the assumption that
u_{2}
is real-valued, so that
\overline{\mathcal{F}(u_{2})}(k)=\mathcal{F}(u_{2})(-k)
.
Then for a smooth vector field
u:\mathbb{T}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the helicity
H(u)
as defined in (
1.1
) is precisely
H(u,u)
. It is clear that
H(u_{1},u_{2})
makes sense if
u_{1},u_{2}\in\dot{H}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
, or if
u_{1}
is smooth and
u_{2}
is a distribution. On the other hand, observe that the sum in (
1.6
), evaluated for a given non-smooth vector field
u=u_{1}=u_{2}
, may converge if
\mathcal{F}(u)(k)
is orthogonal to
k\times\mathcal{F}(u)(-k)
. We therefore offer the following definition of helicity in terms of mollifications and Littlewood-Paley projections, which allows for cancellations in the sum in (
1.6
). At a heuristic level, we require the value of the helicity to be independent of the choice of mollification kernel used in the approximation (c.f. (
1.7
)), and the shell-by-shell helicity to be absolutely summable for every choice of kernel defining the Littlewood-Paley shells, (c.f. (
1.8
)).

Definition 1.1

(
Generalized helicity
)
.

Let
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
be given. We say that the generalized helicity of
u
is well-defined if the following two conditions hold.

(i)

There exists a number
H(u)\in\mathbb{R}
such that for all Schwartz kernels
\varphi
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi=1
,

\displaystyle H(u)=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}H(\varphi_{\epsilon}\ast u,u)\,,\qquad\textnormal{where }\varphi_{\epsilon}(x)=\epsilon^{-3}\varphi(\epsilon^{-1}x)\,.

(1.7)

(ii)

Let
\mathcal{A}
be the class of functions
\widetilde{\varphi}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow[0,1]
which are smooth, compactly supported, and satisfy the following: there exists
\delta(\widetilde{\varphi})>0
, depending on
\widetilde{\varphi}
, such that
\widetilde{\varphi}(x)=1
for all
|x|\leq\delta(\widetilde{\varphi})
. For a given such
\widetilde{\varphi}
, define
\varphi(k)=\widetilde{\varphi}(k)-\widetilde{\varphi}(2k)
and
\varphi_{2^{m}}(k)=\varphi(2^{-m}k)
for
m\geq 1
, and
\varphi_{2^{0}}(k)=\widetilde{\varphi}(k)
. For all such
\varphi
,

\displaystyle\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{m,m^{\prime}=0}^{M}\left|H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}}(u),\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}}(u)\right)\right|<\infty\,,

(1.8)

Remark 1.2

(
Computing helicity shell-by-shell
)
.

If
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
satisfies Definition
1.1
, then for all
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathcal{A}
,

\displaystyle H(u)=\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}H(\check{\widetilde{\varphi}}_{2^{-M}}\ast u,u)=\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{M}H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}}(u),u\right)=\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{m,m^{\prime}=0}^{M}H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}}(u),\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}}(u)\right)\,.

Remark 1.3

(
Comparison with the classical definitions of helicity
)
.

Note that for
\dot{H}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
vector fields, (
1.8
) holds, and the limit in (
1.7
) is well-defined and agrees with (
1.6
) and (
1.1
). Therefore we are justified in using the same notation
H
in all three cases. However, Definition
1.1
does extend the classical definitions to a larger class of objects. For example, shear flows with regularity worse that
\dot{H}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
will satisfy (
1.7
)-(
1.8
) since
u
and
\mathrm{curl\,}u
are always orthogonal (as are any combination of Littlewood-Paley projections or mollifications of
u
and
\mathrm{curl\,}u
). Since helicity is a measure of the total amount of linking, twisting, and writhing of vortex lines, the helicity of a shear flow
should
be zero; the trajectories are all straight lines. More generally, functions which have cancellations of helicity occurring within the overlap of the
k
and
k^{\prime}
shells may fall under the purview of Definition
1.1
, such as functions which in each Littlewood-Paley shell resemble shear flows to leading order. Our construction of weak Euler solutions in this paper will be flows of this type; in each Littlewood-Paley shell, the solution is very nearly a sum of shears.

Our main theorem can now be stated as follows.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
0<\beta<\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
and
T>0
. Suppose that smooth functions
e:[0,T]\rightarrow(0,\infty)
and
h:[0,T]\rightarrow(-\infty,\infty)
are given. Then there exist weak solutions
u:[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
to the 3D Euler equations, belonging to
C([0,T];(H^{\beta}\cap L^{\frac{1}{1-2\beta}})(\mathbb{T}^{3}))
, such that the generalized helicity is well-defined in the sense of Definition
1.1
and the kinetic energy and generalized helicity satisfy

\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\left\|u(t,\cdot)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}^{2}=e(t)\,,\qquad H(u(t))=h(t)\,.

(1.9)

The constructed solutions exhibit intermittency, a characteristic feature of turbulence as in
[
45
]
.
By interpolation, in particular, the solutions belong to
C_{t}B^{s}_{3,\infty,x}
with
s\to\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}
as
\beta\to\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
, and so such solutions are almost Onsager-critical in
L^{3}
.
Using techniques which are by now standard, we could augment the proof of this statement to instead prove non-uniqueness for the initial value problem, for example in a class of functions whose generalized helicities are constant in time. In other words, imposing the conservation of generalized helicity has no bearing whatsoever on the uniqueness of the initial value problem, or on the behavior of the kinetic energy.

1.3 Ideas of the proof

Convex integration and Nash iteration techniques were first applied to the Euler equations by De Lellis and Székelyhidi
[
20
,
21
]
. In the following years, Nash iterations were used to prove Onsager’s conjecture
[
33
,
5
,
31
]
and demonstrate non-uniqueness of weak solutions to the Navier-Stokes equations
[
7
]
. We also refer to the surveys
[
7
,
22
]
for overviews and the papers
[
37
,
40
,
12
,
4
,
19
,
15
,
34
,
27
,
28
,
10
,
11
,
39
,
38
]
for recent developments. On the other hand, our construction of solutions to the Euler equations is based on the style of intermittent Nash iterations pioneered in
[
6
,
45
]
, together with the simplifying ideas of the ‘continuous scheme’ in
[
29
,
30
]
. These tools construct solutions with regularity
L^{\infty}_{t}(H^{1/2-}_{x}\cap L^{\infty-}_{x})
, which is almost enough for the helicity to be well-defined. In order for the generalized helicity to be well-defined and prescribed, we require some new ideas and adaptations of the tools from
[
6
,
45
,
29
,
30
]
.

1.3.1 Mikado flows have zero helicity

The high-frequency building blocks that we use in our Nash iteration are intermittent pipe bundles, or products of two sets of intermittent Mikado flows with different thicknesses and spacings. These are non-interacting, smooth shear flows and so have zero (generalized) helicity. Pretending for a moment that the bundles need not be transported by the low-frequency background flow, the leading order term in the helicity of the perturbation vanishes. By using this cancellation, we are able to control the leading order terms required to satisfy Definition
1.1
. However, a number of further ideas are required to control the helicity from the remaining terms; it is at this point that the methods from
[
6
,
45
,
29
,
30
]
cease to be sufficient.

1.3.2 Shortening of the time-scale

In order for our perturbation to have interact well with the background flow
\Phi
, it is necessary for the high-frequency building blocks to be transported by
\Phi
. If
\Phi
deforms our shear building blocks to order one by an arbitrary diffeomorphism, i.e. if
\|D\Phi-\mathrm{Id}\|=O(1)
, then it is no longer possible to say that the deformed shears have small helicity.

To remedy this, we shorten the time-scale on which we run the background flow from the Lipschitz time-scale
\tau_{q}
to a new time-scale
\mu_{q}\ll\tau_{q}
. Crucially, since the Lipschitz norm of the background vector field is unchanged, we now have
\|D\Phi-\mathrm{Id}\|<\tau_{q}^{-1}\mu_{q}
; see Corollary
5.4
. The helicity contribution from the deformed shears, computed in (
6.19
), is of order
\tau_{q}^{-1}\mu_{q}\lambda_{q}\delta_{q+{\bar{n}}}

which must be
\ll 1
; see subsection
2.1
for the definitions of these parameters. But we cannot choose
\mu_{q}
arbitrarily small due to the transport error (see Section
7.3
and (
8.5a
)), which requires that

\frac{\delta_{q+{\bar{n}}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mu_{q}^{-1}}{\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}}}\frac{\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{{\bar{n}}}}{{2}}}}{\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}}}<\delta_{q+2{\bar{n}}}\,.

Putting the above two constraints together, we see that
\mu_{q}
must satisfy

\delta_{q+2{\bar{n}}}^{-1}\delta_{q+{\bar{n}}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{{\bar{n}}}}{{2}}}\lambda_{{q+{\bar{n}}}}^{-2}<\mu_{q}<\tau_{q}\lambda_{q}^{-1}\delta_{q+{\bar{n}}}^{-1}\underset{\eqref{eq:defn:tau}}{\approx}{\delta_{q+{\bar{n}}}^{-1}\delta_{q}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}^{-2}}\,.

Using the definitions of
\delta_{q}
and
\lambda_{q}
, such a choice of
\mu_{q}
only exists if

\delta_{q+2{\bar{n}}}^{-1}\delta_{q+{\bar{n}}}^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{{\bar{n}}}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}^{2}\lambda_{{q+{\bar{n}}}}^{-2}<1\iff 2\beta\bar{b}^{4}-3\beta\bar{b}^{2}-\beta+2+\bar{b}-2\bar{b}^{2}<0\,.

In other words, we must have
\beta>\frac{2+\bar{b}-2\bar{b}^{2}}{1+3\bar{b}^{2}-2\bar{b}^{4}}
, which is essentially one of the constraints in (
2.1
).

1.3.3 Using regularity to deal with lower order terms

Certain lower order terms, such as the divergence correctors of the principal Nash perturbation, have no apparent cancellations that would imply that their helicity is small. For such terms, we simply estimate their helicity by their amplitude. For example, we estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}w^{(c)}\cdot\mathrm{curl\,}w^{(c)}\,dx\leq\|w^{(c)}\|_{L^{2}}\|Dw^{(c)}\|_{L^{2}}

Such terms can only be small if the solutions are of a certain regularity
\beta>\frac{2-\bar{b}}{2\bar{b}}
; see again (
2.1
).

The oscillation error in the Nash iteration puts an upper bound
\beta<(2\bar{b}^{2})^{-1}
, implying that
(\lambda_{q}\delta_{{q+{\bar{n}}}})_{q=1}^{\infty}
is an increasing sequence. However, the shortening of the time-scale and the lower order terms force lower bounds on the regularity
\beta
in terms of
\bar{b}
, a consequence of which we have that
(\lambda_{q}\delta_{q+2{\bar{n}}})_{q=1}^{\infty}
is a decreasing sequence. For a choice of
\beta
, such bounds are satisfied for a range of
\bar{b}
, but we are unable to send
\bar{b}
to
1
for an arbitrary choice of
\beta<1/2
. We deal with this by first choosing
\bar{b}
and then choosing
\beta
in our Nash iteration. The precise choices are given in Section
2.1
.

1.3.4 New ‘helical’ building blocks in order to inject a desired helicity

In order to prescribe the helicity profile in Theorem
1.4
, at each step of our iteration we add in a new perturbation
w_{h}
that makes sure that the new total helicity differs from our desired profile by an amount that goes to zero along the iteration; see Hypothesis
2.6
for the exact estimate. Our
w_{h}
is small enough in
L^{2}
so that the oscillation errors it produces in the Nash iteration can be directly absorbed into the new Reynolds error without needing to invert the divergence. However,
w_{h}
is large enough in
\dot{H}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
in order to correct the helicity produced by the previous steps of the iteration as well as that produced by the Nash perturbation.

This perturbation
w_{h}
will have the overall structure of the usual Nash perturbation with the intermittent pipe bundles replaced by helical building blocks. These are divergence-free flows that are supported on a small neighborhood of a helix
t\mapsto(r\cos(2\pi\lambda_{*}t),r\sin(2\pi\lambda_{*}t),t)
that itself is (up to fixed geometric constants) contained in the support of a single Mikado. The latter property allows for the required dodging Hypotheses
2.3
–
2.4
to hold using the same proof that these hypotheses hold for the usual Nash perturbation. For this, one must have
r\lesssim\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}}^{-1}
, the radius of a single Mikado; in fact we will choose
r\approx\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}}
. The ‘wavelength’
\lambda_{*}^{-1}
of the helix is chosen such that
\lambda_{*}\ll\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}}
, so that the helical perturbation lives at frequency
\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}}
. However, in order to inject enough helicity and close various estimates, we also require that
\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}-1}\ll\lambda_{*}
.

2
Inductive assumptions

In this section, we present the inductive assumptions required for the iterative proposition, which is stated in subsection
2.8
. The main theorems are then proved in the same subsection.

2.1 General notations and parameters

To formulate the inductive assumptions, we need a number of carefully chosen parameters. We specify the primary parameters now while ignoring certain secondary parameters. A full delineation of the parameters is contained in Section
8
.

(i)

Choose a parameter
\overline{b}
such that
0<\overline{b}-1\ll 1
,
\bar{b}^{2}<2
, and

\displaystyle\max\left(\frac{2+\overline{b}-2\overline{b}^{2}}{1+3\overline{b}^{2}-2\overline{b}^{4}},\frac{2-\overline{b}}{2\overline{b}}\right)<\frac{1}{2\overline{b}^{2}}.

(2.1)

(ii)

We define the regularity index
0<\beta<\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
, so that
\beta\nearrow\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
as
\overline{b}\searrow 1
, by

\beta=\frac{1}{2}\left(\max\left(\frac{2+\overline{b}-2\overline{b}^{2}}{1+3\overline{b}^{2}-2\overline{b}^{4}},\frac{2-\overline{b}}{2\overline{b}}\right)+\frac{1}{2\overline{b}^{2}}\right)

(2.2)

(iii)

Choose
{\bar{n}}\gg 1
an even integer such that

\beta<\frac{1}{2b^{{\bar{n}}}}-\frac{8}{{\bar{n}}}\,,

(2.3)

and then define

b=\overline{b}^{\nicefrac{{2}}{{{\bar{n}}}}}\qquad\iff\qquad\overline{b}=b^{\nicefrac{{{\bar{n}}}}{{2}}}\,.

(2.4)

Since
\overline{b}
is fixed,
b\searrow 1
as
{\bar{n}}\nearrow\infty
.

The frequency parameter
\lambda_{q}
, the amplitude parameter
\delta_{q}
, the intermittency parameter
r_{q}
, and the multi-purpose parameter
\Gamma_{q}
are defined by

\displaystyle\lambda_{q}=2^{\left\lceil(b^{q})\log_{2}a\right\rceil}\approx a^{(b^{q})}\,,\qquad\delta_{q}=\lambda_{0}^{2\beta}\lambda_{q}^{-2\beta}\,,

(2.5a)

\displaystyle r_{q}=\frac{\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{{\bar{n}}}}{{2}}}\Gamma_{q}}{\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}}}

\displaystyle\,,\qquad\Gamma_{q}=2^{\left\lceil\varepsilon_{\Gamma}\log_{2}\left(\frac{\lambda_{q+1}}{\lambda_{q}}\right)\right\rceil}\approx\left(\frac{\lambda_{q+1}}{\lambda_{q}}\right)^{\varepsilon_{\Gamma}}\approx\lambda_{q}^{(b-1)\varepsilon_{\Gamma}}\,.

(2.5b)

We shall require the additional parameters
\tau_{q}
,
\mu_{q}
,
\lambda_{q*}
,
\Lambda_{q}
,
\mathrm{T}_{q}
,
\lambda_{q*}
, and
\mathsf{C_{\infty}}
, defined by

\displaystyle\tau_{q}=\left(\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q}\Gamma_{q}^{35}\right)^{-1},\qquad\mu_{q}={\tau_{q}}\lambda_{q}^{-(1-2\beta b^{{\bar{n}}})}{\Gamma_{q+1}^{-30}}\approx\tau_{q}(\lambda_{q}\delta_{q+{\bar{n}}})^{-1}\Gamma_{q+1}^{{-30}}\,,

(2.6a)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{q}=\lambda_{q}\Gamma_{q}^{10}\,,\qquad\mathrm{T}_{q}={\Lambda_{q}^{10}}\,,\qquad\lambda_{q*}=\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}}\Gamma_{q-100}^{-\frac{1}{100}}\,,\qquad\mathsf{C_{\infty}}{\approx\log_{\Gamma_{q}}\lambda_{q}^{\frac{12}{{\bar{n}}}}}\,,

(2.6b)

so that
\Gamma_{q}^{\mathsf{C_{\infty}}}\leq\lambda_{q}^{\frac{12}{{\bar{n}}}}
. Note that from (
8.5j
), we have that
\mu_{q}\ll\tau_{q}
; the amount by which we shorten
\mu_{q}
is essentially the “amount of extra room in the transport error estimate.” Finally, we set

\displaystyle D_{t,q}=\partial_{t}+\widehat{u}_{q}\cdot\nabla,\quad 1\ll\mathsf{N}_{\rm cut,t}\ll\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}}\ll\mathsf{N}_{\rm ind}\ll\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}

and

\mathcal{M}\left(n,N_{*},\tau^{-1},\mathrm{T}^{-1}\right):=\tau^{-\min\{n,N_{*}\}}\mathrm{T}^{-\max\{n-N_{*},0\}}\,.

Remark 2.1

(
Space-time norms).

We shall always measure objects using uniform-in-time norms
\sup_{t\in[T_{1},T_{2}]}\|\cdot(t)\|
, where
\|\cdot(t)\|
is any of a variety of norms used to measure functions defined on
\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[T_{1},T_{2}]
but restricted to time
t
. We shall always abbreviate these norms by
\|\cdot\|
.

Remark 2.2

(
Space-time balls).

For any set
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}
, we shall use the notations

\displaystyle B(\Omega,\lambda^{-1})

\displaystyle:=\left\{(x,t)\,:\,\exists\,(x_{0},t)\in\Omega\textnormal{ with }|x-x_{0}|\leq\lambda^{-1}\right\}

(2.7a)

\displaystyle B(\Omega,\lambda^{-1},\tau)

\displaystyle:=\left\{(x,t)\,:\,\exists\,(x_{0},t_{0})\in\Omega\textnormal{ with }|x-x_{0}|\leq\lambda^{-1}\,,|t-t_{0}|\leq\tau\right\}

(2.7b)

for space and space-time neighborhoods of
\Omega
of radius
\lambda^{-1}
in space and
\tau
in time, respectively.

2.2 Relaxed equations

We assume that
(u_{q},p_{q},R_{q})
satisfy

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u_{q}+\mathrm{div}(u_{q}\otimes u_{q})+{\nabla p_{q}}=\mathrm{div}R_{q}\,,\qquad\qquad\mathrm{div}\,u_{q}=0\,.\end{cases}

(2.8)

We will use the notations

u_{q}=\underbrace{\widehat{u}_{q-1}+\widehat{w}_{q}}_{=:\widehat{u}_{q}}+\widehat{w}_{q+1}+\dots+\widehat{w}_{q+{\bar{n}}-1}=:\widehat{u}_{q+{\bar{n}}-1}\,

(2.9)

for velocity fields. We assume that
R_{q}
has a decomposition into symmetric matrices

\displaystyle R_{q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=q}^{q+{\bar{n}}-1}R_{q}^{k}\,.

(2.10)

2.3 Inductive assumptions for velocity cutoff functions

We note that all assumptions in subsection
2.3
are assumed to hold for all
q-1\leq q^{\prime}\leq q+{\bar{n}}-1
. First, we assume that the velocity cutoff functions form a partition of unity:

\displaystyle\sum_{i\geq 0}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}^{2}\equiv 1,\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}\psi_{i^{\prime},q^{\prime}}=0\quad\textnormal{for}\quad|i-i^{\prime}|\geq 2\,.

(2.11)

Second, we assume that there exists an
{i_{\rm max}}={i_{\rm max}}(q^{\prime})\geq 0
, bounded uniformly in
q^{\prime}
, such that

\displaystyle{i_{\rm max}}(q^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathsf{C_{\infty}}+12}{(b-1)\varepsilon_{\Gamma}}\,,

(2.12a)

\displaystyle\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}\equiv 0\quad\mbox{for all}\quad i>{i_{\rm max}}(q^{\prime})\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\mbox{and}\qquad\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{{i_{\rm max}}(q^{\prime})}\leq\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-{\bar{n}}}^{\nicefrac{{\mathsf{C_{\infty}}}}{{2}}+18}\delta_{q^{\prime}}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}r_{q^{\prime}-{\bar{n}}}^{-1}\,.

(2.12b)

For all
0\leq i\leq{i_{\rm max}}
, we assume the following pointwise derivative bounds for the cutoff functions
\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}
. First, for multi-indices
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{k}
,
k\geq 0
,
0\leq|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}
, we assume that

\displaystyle\frac{{\bf 1}_{\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}}}{\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}^{1-(K+M)/\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}\left|\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}D^{\alpha_{l}}D_{t,q^{\prime}-1}^{\beta_{l}}\right)\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}\right|\leq\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}(\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}\lambda_{q^{\prime}})^{|\alpha|}\mathcal{M}\left(|\beta|,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}}-\mathsf{N}_{\rm cut,t},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}^{i+3}{\mu_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)\,.

(2.13)

Next, with
\alpha,\beta,k
as above,
N\geq 0
and
D_{q^{\prime}}:=\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}}\cdot\nabla
, we assume that

\displaystyle\frac{{\bf 1}_{\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}}}{\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}^{1-(N+K+M)/\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}\left|D^{N}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}D_{q^{\prime}}^{\alpha_{l}}D_{t,q^{\prime}-1}^{\beta_{l}}\right)\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}(\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}\lambda_{q^{\prime}})^{N}(\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i-5}{\mu_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}})^{|\alpha|}\mathcal{M}\left(|\beta|,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}}-\mathsf{N}_{\rm cut,t},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}^{i+3}{\mu_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)

(2.14)

for
0\leq N+|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}
. Moreover, for
0\leq i\leq{i_{\rm max}}(q^{\prime})
, we assume that

\displaystyle\left\|\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}\right\|_{1}\leq\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{-2i+{\mathsf{C}_{b}}}\qquad\mbox{where}\qquad{\mathsf{C}_{b}}=\frac{4+b}{b-1}\,.

(2.15)

Our last assumption is that for all
q^{\prime}\leq q+{\bar{n}}-1
and all
q^{\prime\prime}\leq q^{\prime}-1
, we assume

\psi_{i^{\prime},q^{\prime}}\psi_{i^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime\prime}}\not\equiv 0\quad\implies\quad\mu_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{-i^{\prime}}\leq\mu_{q^{\prime\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime\prime}}^{-i^{\prime\prime}-25}\,.

(2.16)

2.4 Inductive bounds on the Reynolds stress

We assume that for
q\leq k\leq q+{\bar{n}}-1
and
N+M\leq 2\mathsf{N}_{\rm ind}
,
R_{q}^{k}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\psi_{i,k-1}D^{N}D_{t,k-1}^{M}R_{q}^{k}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{q}\delta_{k+{\bar{n}}}\Lambda_{k}^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{k-1}^{i{+20}}{\mu_{k-1}^{-1}},\mathrm{T}_{k-1}^{-1}{\Gamma_{k-1}^{10}}\right)\,.

(2.17a)

\displaystyle\left\|\psi_{i,k-1}D^{N}D_{t,k-1}^{M}R_{q}^{k}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{q}{\Gamma_{k}^{\mathsf{C_{\infty}}}}\Lambda_{k}^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{k-1}^{i{+20}}{\mu_{k-1}^{-1}},\mathrm{T}_{k-1}^{-1}{\Gamma_{k-1}^{10}}\right)\,,

(2.17b)

2.5 Dodging inductive hypotheses

Hypothesis 2.3

(
Effective dodging).

For
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}\leq q+{\bar{n}}-1
that satisfy
0<|q^{\prime\prime}-q^{\prime}|\leq{\bar{n}}-1
,

B\left(\mathrm{supp\,}\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}},\lambda_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}+1}\right)\cap B\left(\mathrm{supp\,}\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime\prime}},\lambda_{q^{\prime\prime}}^{-1}\Gamma_{q^{\prime\prime}+1}\right)=\emptyset\,.

(2.18)

Hypothesis 2.4

(
Density and direction of old pipe bundles
)
.

There exists a
q
-independent constant
\mathcal{C}_{D}
such that the following holds. Let
\bar{q}^{\prime},\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}
satisfy
q\leq\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}<\bar{q}^{\prime}\leq q+{\bar{n}}-1
, and set

d(\bar{q}^{\prime},\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}):=\min\left[(\lambda_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}\Gamma_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}^{7})^{-1},(\lambda_{\bar{q}^{\prime}-\nicefrac{{{\bar{n}}}}{{2}}}\Gamma_{\bar{q}^{\prime}-{\bar{n}}})^{-1}\right]\,.

(2.19)

Let
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
be any time and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{T}^{3}
be a convex set of diameter at most
d(\bar{q}^{\prime},\bar{q}^{\prime\prime})
. Let
i
be such that
\Omega\times\{t_{0}\}\cap\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}\neq\emptyset
.
Let
\Phi_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}
be the flow map such that

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\Phi_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}+\left(\widehat{u}_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}\cdot\nabla\right)\Phi_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}=0\\
\Phi_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}(t_{0},x)=x\,.\end{cases}

We define
\Omega(t)=\Phi_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}(t)^{-1}(\Omega)
.
1
1

1

For any set
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{T}^{3}
,
\Phi_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}(t)^{-1}(\Omega^{\prime})=\{x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}:\Phi_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}(t,x)\in\Omega^{\prime}\}
. We shall also sometimes write
\Omega\circ\Phi_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}(t)
.

Then there exists a set
L=L(\bar{q}^{\prime},\bar{q}^{\prime\prime},\Omega,{t_{0}})\subseteq\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}
such that for all
t\in(t_{0}-{\mu_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}}\Gamma_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}^{-i+2},t_{0}+{\mu_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}}\Gamma_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}^{-i+2})
,

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+\widehat{u}_{\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{1}_{L}(t,\cdot)\equiv 0\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\textnormal{supp}_{x}\widehat{w}_{\bar{q}^{\prime}}(x,t)\cap\Omega(t)\subseteq L\cap\{t\}\,.

(2.20)

Furthermore, there exists a finite family of continuously differentiable curves
\{\ell_{j,L}\}_{j=1}^{\mathcal{C}_{D}}
of length at most
2d(\bar{q}^{\prime},\bar{q}^{\prime\prime})
which satisfy

L\cap\{t=t_{0}\}\subseteq\bigcup_{j=1}^{\mathcal{C}_{D}}B\left(\ell_{j,L},3\lambda_{\bar{q}^{\prime}}^{-1}\right)\,.

(2.21)

Finally, there exist
q
-independent sets
\Xi_{0},\Xi_{0}^{\prime},\dots,\Xi_{{\bar{n}}-1},\Xi_{{\bar{n}}-1}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{Q}^{3}\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}
such that for all curves
\ell_{j,L}
used to control the support of
\widehat{w}_{\overline{q}^{\prime}}
, the tangent vector to the curve
\ell_{j,L}
belongs to a
\Gamma_{{0}}^{-1}
-neighborhood of a vector
\xi\in\Xi_{\overline{q}^{\prime}\,\textnormal{mod}\,{\bar{n}}}\cup\Xi^{\prime}_{\overline{q}^{\prime}\,\textnormal{mod}\,{\bar{n}}}
.

2.6 Inductive velocity bounds

In this subsection, we assume that
{1}\leq q^{\prime}\leq q+{\bar{n}}-1
. First, for
0\leq i\leq{i_{\rm max}}
,
k\geq 1
,
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, we assume that

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}D^{\alpha_{l}}D_{t,q^{\prime}-1}^{\beta_{l}}\right)\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}})}\leq\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i+2}\delta_{q^{\prime}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}})^{|\alpha|}\mathcal{M}\left(|\beta|,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{{q^{\prime}}}^{i+3}{\mu_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}},\Gamma_{{q^{\prime}-1}}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)

(2.22)

for
|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq{\nicefrac{{3\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}{{2}}+1}
. We also assume that for
N\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\left\|D^{N}\Big(\prod_{l=1}^{k}D_{q^{\prime}}^{\alpha_{l}}D_{t,q^{\prime}-1}^{\beta_{l}}\Big)\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq(\Gamma_{{q^{\prime}}}^{i+2}\delta_{q^{\prime}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}})^{|\alpha|+1}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}})^{N+|\alpha|}\mathcal{M}\left(|\beta|,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i+3}{\mu_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)

(2.23a)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i+2}\delta_{q^{\prime}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}})^{N}(\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i-5}{\mu_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}})^{|\alpha|}\mathcal{M}\left(|\beta|,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i+3}{\mu_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)

(2.23b)

whenever
N+|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq{\nicefrac{{3\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}{{2}}+1}
. Next, we assume

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}D^{\alpha_{l}}D_{t,q^{\prime}}^{\beta_{l}}\right)D\widehat{u}_{q^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}})}\leq{\delta_{q^{\prime}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i+3}}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}})^{|\alpha|}\mathcal{M}\left(|\beta|,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i-5}{\mu_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)

(2.24)

for
|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq{\nicefrac{{3\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}{{2}}}
. In addition, we assume the lossy bounds

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}D^{\alpha_{l}}D_{t,q^{\prime}}^{\beta_{l}}\right)\widehat{u}_{q^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i+2}\delta_{q^{\prime}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lambda_{q^{\prime}}^{2}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}})^{|\alpha|}\mathcal{M}\left(|\beta|,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i-5}{\mu_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)

(2.25a)

\displaystyle\left\|D^{|\alpha|}\partial_{t}^{|\beta|}\widehat{u}_{q^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{q^{\prime}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}})^{|\alpha|}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}}^{-|\beta|}\,,

(2.25b)

hold, where the first bound holds for
|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq\nicefrac{{3\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}{{2}}+1
, and the second bound for
|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq 2\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}
.

Remark 2.5

(
Upgrading material derivatives for velocity and velocity cutoffs).

Similar to
[
29
, Remark 2.9]
, we have the bound

\displaystyle\left\|D^{N}D_{t,q^{\prime}}^{M}\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}})}\lesssim\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i+2}\delta_{q^{\prime}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}})^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i-5}{\mu_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)

(2.26)

for all
N+M\leq{\nicefrac{{3\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}{{2}}+1}
. We also have that for all
N+M\leq\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}
,

\displaystyle\frac{{\bf 1}_{\mathrm{supp\,}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}}}{\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}^{1-(N+M)/\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}\left|D^{N}D_{t,q^{\prime}}^{M}\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}})^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}}-\mathsf{N}_{\rm cut,t},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i-5}{\mu_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle<\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}(\lambda_{q^{\prime}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}})^{N}\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{i-4}{\mu_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}},\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}^{2}\mathrm{T}_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\right)\,.

(2.27)

2.7 Inductive energy and helicity bounds

We assume that

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{q+{\bar{n}}}\Gamma_{q}^{10}}{4}\leq e(t)-\frac{1}{2}\|u_{q}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\delta_{q+{\bar{n}}}\Gamma_{q}^{10}\,.

(2.28)

Let
B=\overline{B_{1}(0)}\subset\mathbb{C}
be the closed unit ball in the complex plane. For any
q^{\prime}\leq q
, the helicity of
u_{q^{\prime}}
satisfies the following hypothesis.

Hypothesis 2.6

(
Helicity prescription).

For any
q^{\prime}\leq q
and any multiplier
\varphi:\mathbb{Z}^{3}\rightarrow B
satisfying
\varphi(k)=1
for
|k|\leq\lambda_{q^{\prime}+{\bar{n}}-1}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}+{\bar{n}}-1}
, we have that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}\leq h(t)-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\mathbb{P}_{\varphi}(u_{q^{\prime}})\cdot\mathrm{curl\,}u_{q^{\prime}}\leq\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\,.

(2.29)

Note we may take
\varphi\equiv 1
above, so that (
2.29
) implies that for all
q^{\prime}\leq q
,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}\leq h(t)-H(u_{q^{\prime}})\leq\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-1}^{-1}\,.

(2.30)

Hypothesis 2.7

(
Helicity-in-a-shell).

For
q^{\prime}\leq q
and any Schwartz kernel
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow B
satisfying

\displaystyle\|\check{\varphi}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq 1\,,\quad\left\|\mathbf{1}_{F_{q}}\check{\varphi}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq\lambda_{q^{\prime}+{\bar{n}}}^{-100}\,,\quad\textnormal{where }F_{q}=\left(B_{(\lambda_{q^{\prime}+{\bar{n}}-1}\Gamma^{-2}_{q^{\prime}+{\bar{n}}-1})^{-1}}\right)^{c}\,,

(2.31)

we have that
2
2

2

We have in mind
\mathbb{P}_{\varphi}=\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}}\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}}=\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}*\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}}

; this condition will allow us to prove (
1.8
).

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\mathbb{P}_{\varphi}(\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}+{\bar{n}}-1})\cdot\mathrm{curl\,}\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}+{\bar{n}}-1}\right|<\Gamma_{q^{\prime}-5}^{-1/2}\,.

(2.32)

Hypothesis 2.8

(
Frequency localization).

For any
q^{\prime}\leq q
, any natural numbers
k_{1}\leq\lambda_{q^{\prime}+{\bar{n}}-1}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}+{\bar{n}}-1}^{-1}
and
k_{2}\geq\lambda_{q^{\prime}+{\bar{n}}-1}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}+{\bar{n}}-1}
, and any multipliers
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}:\mathbb{Z}^{3}\rightarrow B
such that
\varphi_{1}(k)=0
for all
|k|>k_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}(j)=0
for all
|j|<k_{2}
,
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{1}}\left(\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}+{\bar{n}}-1}\right)\right\|_{\infty}\leq{k_{1}}{\lambda_{q^{\prime}+{\bar{n}}-1}^{-20}}\,,\qquad\left\|\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2}}\left(\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}+{\bar{n}}-1}\right)\right\|_{\infty}\leq{\lambda_{q^{\prime}+{\bar{n}}-1}}{k_{2}^{-20}}\,.

(2.33)

Hypothesis 2.9

(
Temporal regularity).

For
0\leq M\leq\nicefrac{{\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}}}{{3}}
,
H(u_{q}):{[0,T]}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfies

\left\|\left[\nicefrac{{d}}{{dt}}\right]^{M}H(u_{q})\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}\lesssim\mathcal{M}\left(M,\mathsf{N}_{\textnormal{ind,t}},\mu_{q-1}^{-1}\Gamma_{q-1}^{20},\mathrm{T}_{q-1}^{-1}\Gamma_{q-1}^{8}\right)\,.

(2.34)

2.8 Iterative proposition and proof of main theorem

and

Iterative proposition).

,
\mathsf{N}_{\rm ind}
,
\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}
such that we can find sufficiently large

such that for

, the following statements hold for any

. Suppose that
(u_{q},p_{q},R_{q})
solves (

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

In
Step 0
, we construct initial approximate solution
(u_{1},p_{1},R_{1})
.
Then, applying Proposition
2.10
iteratively, we obtain a sequence of approximate solutions and construct a solution with desired regularity and total kinetic energy in
Step 1
.
In
Step 2
, we check that (
1.8
) is satisfied; that is, the helicity is absolutely summable over any choice of Littlewood-Paley decomposition. In
Step 3
, we check (
1.7
) and the prescription of the helicity profile
h(t)
.

Step 0: Construct
(u_{1},p_{1},R_{1})
.
We first choose parameters. Let

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}=\lambda_{0},\quad\bar{\lambda}_{*}=\lambda_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}+\frac{1}{3}(b^{{\bar{n}}+1}-1)},\quad\bar{\tau}=\lambda_{0}^{-1}\,,\quad\widehat{u}_{0,R}=\bar{e}(t)\bar{\varrho}_{R}(x)\vec{e}\,.

Here, we choose
a
in the definition of
\lambda_{0}
(see (
2.5a
)) sufficiently large so that
\bar{e}(t):=\sqrt{2e(t)-\delta_{{\bar{n}}+1}}\geq\left(\min_{t\in[0,T]}e(t)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
,
and
\vec{e}:=\frac{3}{5}e_{2}+\frac{4}{5}e_{3}
.
Also,
\bar{\varrho}_{R}=\bar{\lambda}^{-{\mathsf{d}}}\mathrm{div}^{{\mathsf{d}}}\bar{\vartheta}_{R}
, where
\bar{\vartheta}_{R}
is smooth,
(\nicefrac{{\mathbb{T}}}{{\bar{\lambda}}})^{3}
-periodic, has support contained in pipes of thickness
\bar{\lambda}^{-1}
, and satisfies

\displaystyle\vec{e}\cdot\nabla\vartheta_{R}=0,\quad\left\|\nabla^{N}\bar{\vartheta}_{R}\right\|_{\infty}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}^{N},\quad\left\|\bar{\varrho}_{R}\right\|_{2}^{2}=1

for all
N\leq 2\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}+{\mathsf{d}}
. This implies that
\widehat{u}_{0,R}
solves stationary Euler with zero pressure and satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{u}_{0,R}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}^{2}=2e(t)-\delta_{\bar{n}},\quad H(\widehat{u}_{0,R})=0\,.

Define
\widehat{u}_{0,h,\pm}
by

\displaystyle\widehat{u}_{0,h,\pm}=\frac{\bar{\lambda}}{\bar{\lambda}_{*}^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}}\bar{h}_{\pm}(t)\bar{\varrho}_{h}(\Psi_{\pm})(\nabla\Psi_{\pm})^{-1}e_{2}\,.

Here,

\bar{h}_{\pm}(t)=\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}}\sqrt{(h(t)-2^{-1}\Gamma_{0}^{-1})_{\pm}}

and
\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}}
is a standard mollifier in time whose kernel is compactly supported on the interval
(-\bar{\tau},\bar{\tau})
. The deformation
\Psi_{\pm}
is defined by

\displaystyle\Psi_{\pm}

\displaystyle=(x_{1}\pm\bar{\lambda}^{-1}\sin(\bar{\lambda}_{*}x_{2}),x_{2},x_{3}+\bar{\lambda}^{-1}\cos(\bar{\lambda}_{*}x_{2}))^{\top}

Lastly, the density functions
\bar{\varrho}_{h}
satisfy the same assumptions as
\bar{\varrho}_{R}
, except that
\nabla\bar{\varrho}_{h}
is orthogonal to
e_{2}
instead of
\vec{e}
. Moreover,

\bar{\varrho}_{R}
and

\bar{\varrho}_{h}
are chosen to have disjoint supports. Then, one can compute

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{u}_{0,h,\pm}\right\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\frac{\bar{\lambda}^{2}}{\bar{\lambda}_{*}^{3}},\quad H(\widehat{u}_{0,h,\pm})=\pm\bar{h}_{\pm}^{2}\,.

Set
\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}}\equiv 0
for
1\leq q^{\prime}\leq{\bar{n}}
,
p_{0}=0
,
u_{1}=\widehat{u}_{0}=\widehat{u}_{0,R}+\widehat{u}_{0,h,+}+\widehat{u}_{0,h,-}=:\widehat{u}_{0,R}+\widehat{u}_{0,h}
,

R_{1}^{k}\equiv 0
for
1<k\leq{\bar{n}}
, and

\displaystyle R_{1}^{1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{u}_{0,h}\otimes\widehat{u}_{0,h}+\frac{\bar{\lambda}}{\bar{\lambda}_{*}^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}}\bar{h}_{+}^{\prime}\mathcal{H}(\bar{\varrho}_{h}(\Psi_{+})(\nabla\Psi_{+})^{-1}e_{2})+\frac{\bar{\lambda}}{\bar{\lambda}_{*}^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}}\bar{h}_{-}^{\prime}\mathcal{H}(\bar{\varrho}_{h}(\Psi_{-})(\nabla\Psi_{-})^{-1}e_{2})

\displaystyle\quad+\bar{e}^{\prime}\begin{pmatrix}0&(\bar{\lambda}^{-{\mathsf{d}}}\mathrm{div}^{{\mathsf{d}}-1}\bar{\vartheta}_{R})_{2}&(\bar{\lambda}^{-{\mathsf{d}}}\mathrm{div}^{{\mathsf{d}}-1}\bar{\vartheta}_{R})_{3}\\
(\bar{\lambda}^{-{\mathsf{d}}}\mathrm{div}^{{\mathsf{d}}-1}\bar{\vartheta}_{R})_{2}&0&0\\
(\bar{\lambda}^{-{\mathsf{d}}}\mathrm{div}^{{\mathsf{d}}-1}\bar{\vartheta}_{R})_{3}&0&0\,,\end{pmatrix}

where
\mathcal{H}
is an operator inverting the divergence as in
[
29
, Proposition A.13]
. Furthermore, set
\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}=1
if
i=0
and
0
otherwise for
0\leq q^{\prime}\leq{\bar{n}}
.

By construction, one can easily check that
(u_{1},p_{1},R_{1})
solves the Euler-Reynolds system and
R_{1}^{k}
are symmetric matrices. Also,
\psi_{i,q^{\prime}}
for
0\leq q^{\prime}\leq{\bar{n}}
satisfies all inductive hypotheses in Section
2.3
for
q=1
. By the choices of
\bar{\lambda}
,
\bar{\lambda}_{*}
, and
\bar{\tau}
so that
\frac{\bar{\lambda}^{2}}{\bar{\lambda}_{*}^{3}}\ll\delta_{{\bar{n}}+1}
and
\bar{\tau}^{-1}\leq\mu_{0}
,
R_{1}^{k}
satisfies the inductive hypotheses in Section
2.4
for
q=0
. Since
\widehat{w}_{\bar{q}^{\prime}}\equiv 0
for
1\leq\bar{q}^{\prime}\leq{\bar{n}}
,
\widehat{u}_{q^{\prime}}=\widehat{u}_{0}
for
0\leq q^{\prime}\leq{\bar{n}}
, and
\bar{\lambda}=\lambda_{0}\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}
, the inductive hypotheses in Section
2.5
and
2.6
hold for
q=1
. Finally, we consider the inductive energy and helicity bounds. Since
\widehat{u}_{0,R}
and
\widehat{u}_{0,h}
have disjoint supports,

\displaystyle\left\|u_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\left\|\widehat{u}_{0,R}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|\widehat{u}_{0,h}\right\|_{2}^{2}=2e(t)-\delta_{{\bar{n}}+1}+O\left(\frac{\bar{\lambda}^{2}}{\bar{\lambda}_{*}^{3}}\right)\,.

Using
\frac{\bar{\lambda}^{2}}{\bar{\lambda}_{*}^{3}}\ll\delta_{{\bar{n}}+1}
, (
2.28
) holds true. As for the helicity,

\displaystyle H(u_{1})

\displaystyle=H(\widehat{u}_{0,R})+H(\widehat{u}_{0,h,+})+H(\widehat{u}_{0,h,-})+2H(\widehat{u}_{0,h,+},\widehat{u}_{0,h,-})=\bar{h}^{2}_{+}-\bar{h}^{2}_{-}+2H(\widehat{u}_{0,h,+},\widehat{u}_{0,h,-})

\displaystyle=g_{+}^{2}-g_{-}^{2}+(\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}}g_{+})^{2}-g_{+}^{2}-\left((\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}}g_{-})^{2}-g_{-}^{2}\right)+2H(\widehat{u}_{0,h,+},\widehat{u}_{0,h,-})

\displaystyle=h(t)-2^{-1}\Gamma_{0}^{-1}+O(\Gamma_{1}^{-1})

(2.35)

where
g_{\pm}:=\sqrt{(h(t)-2^{-1}\Gamma_{0}^{-1})_{\pm}}
.
To prove the last line, we write

\displaystyle|(\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}}-1)g_{\pm}(t)|\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}|K_{\bar{\tau}}(s)||g_{\pm}(t-s)-g_{\pm}(t)|ds

(2.36)

on
[0,T]
, and split into three cases. First if
\left\|h^{\prime}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,T+1])}=0
, then
h
and
g_{\pm}
are constant functions. Therefore

\displaystyle|g_{\pm}(t-s)-g_{\pm}(t)|\equiv 0

(2.37)

for any
t\in[0,T]
and
|s|\leq\bar{\tau}
. Second, we now assume that
\left\|h^{\prime}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,T+1])}>0
and consider
t\in[0,T]
such that
|h(t)-2^{-1}\Gamma_{0}^{-1}|\leq 2\left\|h^{\prime}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,T+1])}\bar{\tau}
. Then for any
|s|\leq\bar{\tau}
,

\displaystyle|h(t-s)-2^{-1}\Gamma_{0}^{-1}|

\displaystyle\leq|h(t-s)-h(t)|+|h(t)-2^{-1}\Gamma_{0}^{-1}|\leq 3\left\|h^{\prime}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,T+1])}\bar{\tau}\,,

\displaystyle|g_{\pm}(t-s)-g_{\pm}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq|g_{\pm}(t-s)|+|g_{\pm}(t)|\lesssim\bar{\tau}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,.

(2.38)

Lastly, if
\left\|h^{\prime}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,T+1])}>0
and and
|h(t)-2^{-1}\Gamma_{0}^{-1}|>2\left\|h^{\prime}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,T+1])}\bar{\tau}
, then we have

\displaystyle|h(t-\tau)-2^{-1}\Gamma_{0}^{-1}|\geq|h(t)-2^{-1}\Gamma_{0}^{-1}|-|h(t-\tau)-h(t)|\geq\left\|h^{\prime}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,T+1])}\bar{\tau}

for any
|\tau|\leq\bar{\tau}
, and

\displaystyle|g_{\pm}(t-s)-g_{\pm}(t)|\leq\int_{0}^{s}|g^{\prime}(t-\tau)|d\tau=\int_{0}^{s}\frac{|h^{\prime}(t-\tau)|}{2\sqrt{(h(t-\tau)-2^{-1}\Gamma_{0}^{-1}})_{\pm}}d\tau\lesssim\frac{\left\|h^{\prime}\right\|_{\infty}\bar{\tau}}{2\sqrt{\left\|h^{\prime}\right\|_{\infty}\bar{\tau}}}\lesssim\bar{\tau}^{\frac{1}{2}}

(2.39)

for any
|s|\leq\bar{\tau}
.
Combining the estimates (
2.36
)–(
2.39
), we get

\displaystyle|(\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}}g_{\pm})^{2}-g_{\pm}^{2})|\leq|(\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}}-1)g_{\pm})||(\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\tau}}+1)g_{\pm})|\lesssim\bar{\tau}^{\frac{1}{2}}\ll\Gamma_{1}^{-1}.

On the other hand, to handle
H(\widehat{u}_{0,h,+},\widehat{u}_{0,h,-})
, we note that the supports of

\bar{h}_{+}
and
\bar{h}_{-}
overlap only on the set
\Omega_{0}:=\bigcup_{\{t_{0}:h(t_{0})=2^{-1}\Gamma_{0}^{-1}\}}[t_{0}-\bar{\tau},t_{0}+\bar{\tau}]
. When
t\in B(\Omega_{0},\bar{\tau}):=\{s:|s-s_{0}|<\bar{\tau},s_{0}\in\Omega_{0}\}
, we have
t\in[t_{0}-2\bar{\tau},t_{0}+2\bar{\tau}]
for some
t_{0}
satisfying
h(t_{0})=2^{-1}\Gamma_{0}^{-1}
, and

\displaystyle|h(t)-2^{-1}\Gamma_{0}^{-1}|\leq|h(t)-h(t_{0})|+|h(t_{0})-2^{-1}\Gamma_{0}^{-1}|\leq 2\left\|h^{\prime}\right\|_{\infty}\bar{\tau}\,.

This implies that

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}^{-N}\left\|\nabla^{N}\widehat{u}_{0,h,\pm}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{0}\times\mathbb{T}^{3})}\lesssim\frac{\bar{\lambda}}{\bar{\lambda}_{*}^{\frac{3}{2}}}\bar{\tau}^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad H(\widehat{u}_{0,h,+},\widehat{u}_{0,h,-})\lesssim\frac{\bar{\lambda}^{3}}{\bar{\lambda}_{*}^{3}}\bar{\tau}\ll\Gamma_{1}^{-1}\,.

Therefore, we verified (
2.35
).

Now, we check the inductive assumptions for the helicity bounds in section
2.7
.
Let
\varphi:\mathbb{Z}^{3}\to[0,1]
be any multiplier which satisfies
\varphi(k)=1
for
|k|\leq\lambda_{{\bar{n}}}\Gamma_{{\bar{n}}}
. Then,

\displaystyle H(u_{1})-\int\mathbb{P}_{\varphi}(u_{1})\cdot\mathrm{curl\,}u_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}(1-\varphi(k))\mathcal{F}(u_{1})(k)\cdot 2\pi ik\times\mathcal{F}(u_{1})(-k)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}(1-\varphi(k))|k|^{-2{\mathsf{d}}}\mathcal{F}(u_{1})(k)\cdot|k|^{2{\mathsf{d}}}2\pi ik\times\mathcal{F}(u_{1})(-k)\,

and

\displaystyle\left|H(u_{1})-\int\mathbb{P}_{\varphi}(u_{1})\cdot\mathrm{curl\,}u_{1}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{k}|(1-\varphi)|^{2}|k|^{-4{\mathsf{d}}}|\mathcal{F}(u_{1})(k)|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|\Delta^{\mathsf{d}}\mathrm{curl\,}u_{1}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\lambda_{{\bar{n}}}\Gamma_{{\bar{n}}})^{-2{\mathsf{d}}}\bar{\lambda}^{2{\mathsf{d}}+1}\leq\frac{1}{10}\Gamma_{0}^{-1}\,.

This together with (
2.35
)
implies (
2.29
) and (
2.30
) when
q^{\prime}=1
. The inequalities (
2.32
) and (
2.33
) with
q^{\prime}=1
hold true because
\widehat{w}_{{\bar{n}}}\equiv 0
. Lastly, (
2.34
) holds for
q=1
because
\bar{\tau}^{-1}\leq\mu_{0}^{-1}
.

Step 1: Constructing a weak Euler solution and prescribing the energy profile
e(t)
.

With the initial approximate solution
(u_{1},p_{1},R_{1})
from
Step 0
at hand, we apply Proposition
2.10
iteratively to obtain a sequence
\{u_{q}\}_{q=1}^{\infty}
of the approximate solutions. Since
\beta=\beta(\bar{b})\nearrow\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
as
\bar{b}\searrow 1
(see (
2.2
)), for any given
\bar{\beta}\in(0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})
,
\bar{\beta}<\beta<\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
can be achieved by adjusting
\bar{b}
. Then, the usual interpolation argument implies that
\{u_{q}\}
is Cauchy in
C_{t}(H^{\bar{\beta}}\cap L^{\frac{1}{1-2\bar{\beta})}})
, leading to the desired regularity of
u:=\lim_{q\rightarrow\infty}u_{q}
. Furthermore,
u
is a weak solution to the Euler equations by
the convergence of
u_{q}
to
u
in
L^{2}_{t,x}
and that of
R_{q}
to
0
in
L^{1}_{t,x}
. Also, the energy prescription, (i.e., the first part of (
1.9
)) follows from (
2.28
) and the convergence of
u_{q}
to
u
in
C^{0}_{t}L^{2}
.

Step 2: Checking (
1.8
).

Let
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathcal{A}
. We first note that since
\widetilde{\varphi}
is compactly supported, there exists
M=M(\varphi)\in\mathbb{N}
such that the Littlewood-Paley kernels
\varphi_{2^{m}}
and
\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}
defined using
\widetilde{\varphi}
have disjoint supports if
|m-m^{\prime}|>M
. We will check that

\sum_{m,m^{\prime}}\left|H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}}(u),\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}}(u)\right)\right|\leq\sum_{m,m^{\prime}}\sum_{q,q^{\prime}}\left|H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}}(\widehat{w}_{q}),\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}}(\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}})\right)\right|<\infty\,.

(2.40)

The first inequality above follows immediately from the triangle inequality and the fact that
u=\sum_{q}\widehat{w}_{q}
, so it only remains to check the second inequality in (
2.40
). We split

\displaystyle\sum_{m,m^{\prime}}\sum_{q,q^{\prime}}\left|H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}}(\widehat{w}_{q}),\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}}(\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}})\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,m^{\prime}}\sum_{q\neq q^{\prime}}\left|H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}}(\widehat{w}_{q}),\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}}(\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}})\right)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{m,m^{\prime}}\sum_{q}\left|H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}}(\widehat{w}_{q}),\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}}(\widehat{w}_{q})\right)\right|

\displaystyle=S_{\neq}+S_{=}\,.

(2.41)

We first show that
S_{\neq}<\infty
. Morally speaking, this term should be finite because
\widehat{w}_{q}
and
\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}}
have frequency supports very far away from one another due to superexponential frequency growth, which combined with the restriction
|m-m^{\prime}|\leq M
and (
2.33
) allows us to bound this term. To make this precise, we first note that due to the superexponential growth of
\lambda_{q}
as specified in (
2.5a
),

\lim_{q\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\lambda_{q}\Gamma_{q}}{\lambda_{q+1}\Gamma_{q+1}^{-1}}=0\,.

As a consequence, there exists
q_{0}
, dependent on the support of
\widetilde{\varphi}
, such that for all
\widetilde{q}\geq q_{0}
and all
q^{\prime\prime}\neq\widetilde{q}
, the following holds. If

\varphi_{2^{m}}
takes non-zero values on frequencies whose magnitude belongs to
[\lambda_{q^{\prime\prime}+{\bar{n}}-1}\Gamma_{q^{\prime\prime}+{\bar{n}}-1}^{-1},\lambda_{q^{\prime\prime}+{\bar{n}}-1}\Gamma_{q^{\prime\prime}+{\bar{n}}-1}]
,
then
\varphi_{2^{m}}(k)=0
for all frequencies
k
such that
|k|\in[\lambda_{\widetilde{q}+{\bar{n}}-1}\Gamma_{\widetilde{q}+{\bar{n}}-1}^{-1},\lambda_{\widetilde{q}+{\bar{n}}-1}\Gamma_{\widetilde{q}+{\bar{n}}-1}]
. Applying this fact to all terms in
S_{\neq}
where either
q
or
q^{\prime}
is larger than
q_{0}
, we find that either
\widehat{w}_{q}
or
\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}}
satisfies one of the estimates from (
2.33
). The same fact applies also to all terms in
S_{\neq}
where
q,q^{\prime}<q_{0}
but either
m>\log_{2}\left(\lambda_{q_{0}+{\bar{n}}}\Gamma_{q_{0}+{\bar{n}}}\right)
or
m^{\prime}>\log_{2}\left(\lambda_{q_{0}+{\bar{n}}}\Gamma_{q_{0}+{\bar{n}}}\right)
. Thus we can write

\displaystyle S_{\neq}\leq\sum_{m,m^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\neq q^{\prime}\\
q,q^{\prime}<q_{0}\end{subarray}}\left|H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}}(\widehat{w}_{q}),\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}}(\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}})\right)\right|+2\sum_{m,m^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\neq q^{\prime}\\
q\geq q_{0}\end{subarray}}\left|H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}}(\widehat{w}_{q}),\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}}(\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}})\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,m^{\prime}\\
m,m^{\prime}\leq\log_{2}\left(\lambda_{q_{0}+{\bar{n}}}\Gamma_{q_{0}+{\bar{n}}}\right)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\neq q^{\prime}\\
q,q^{\prime}<q_{0}\end{subarray}}\left|H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}}(\widehat{w}_{q}),\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}}(\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}})\right)\right|

\displaystyle+2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,m^{\prime}\\
m>\log_{2}\left(\lambda_{q_{0}+{\bar{n}}}\Gamma_{q_{0}+{\bar{n}}}\right)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\neq q^{\prime}\\
q,q^{\prime}<q_{0}\end{subarray}}\left|H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}}(\widehat{w}_{q}),\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}}(\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}})\right)\right|+2\sum_{m,m^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\neq q^{\prime}\\
q\geq q_{0}\end{subarray}}\left|H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}}(\widehat{w}_{q}),\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}}(\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}})\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,m^{\prime}\\
m,m^{\prime}\leq\log_{2}\left(\lambda_{q_{0}+{\bar{n}}}\Gamma_{q_{0}+{\bar{n}}}\right)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\neq q^{\prime}\\
q,q^{\prime}<q_{0}\end{subarray}}\left|H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}}(\widehat{w}_{q}),\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}}(\widehat{w}_{q^{\prime}})\right)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad+2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,m^{\prime}\\
m>\log_{2}\left(\lambda_{q_{0}+{\bar{n}}}\Gamma_{q_{0}+{\bar{n}}}\right)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\neq q^{\prime}\\
q,q^{\prime}<q_{0}\end{subarray}}{\delta_{q^{\prime}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{100}\frac{\lambda_{q}^{2}}{(2^{m})^{2}}}

\displaystyle\qquad+2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,m^{\prime}\\
m\leq\log_{2}\left(\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}-1}\Gamma_{q+{\bar{n}}-1}^{-1}\right)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\neq q^{\prime}\\
q\geq q_{0}\end{subarray}}\frac{(2^{m})^{2}}{\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}-1}^{2}}\delta_{q^{\prime}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{100}+2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,m^{\prime}\\
m\geq\log_{2}\left(\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}-1}\Gamma_{q+{\bar{n}}-1}\right)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\neq q^{\prime}\\
q\geq q_{0}\end{subarray}}\frac{\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}-1}^{2}}{(2^{m})^{2}}\delta_{q^{\prime}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Gamma_{q^{\prime}}^{100}\,.

(2.42)

The first sum on the right-hand side in (
2.42
) contains finitely many terms and is therefore finite. The second contains finitely many different values of
q
and
q^{\prime}
, and then since
|m-m^{\prime}|\leq M
and we have
2^{m}
in the denominator, this term is finite. Next, the third term is summable in
q^{\prime}
, while the summability in
m
and
q
follows from the fact that
\frac{2^{m}}{\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}-1}}\rightarrow 0
superexponentially as
\rightarrow\infty
due to the restriction that
m\leq\log_{2}(\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}-1}\Gamma_{q+{\bar{n}}-1}^{-1})
and the superexponential growth of frequencies (summability in
m^{\prime}
follows from
|m-m^{\prime}|\leq M
). Similarly, the fourth term is summable in
q^{\prime}
, and in
q
and
m
since
\frac{\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}-1}}{2^{m}}\rightarrow 0
superexponentially
as
q\rightarrow\infty
due to the condition
m\geq\log_{2}\left(\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}-1}\Gamma_{q+{\bar{n}}-1}\right)
and the superexponential growth of frequencies. Thus
S_{\neq}<\infty
.

Moving to
S_{=}
, we first consider the cases when

\varphi_{2^{m}}
and
\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}
are supported

outside
the active frequency support of
\widehat{w}_{q}
; that is, when either
\varphi_{2^{m}}
or
\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}
satisfies (
2.33
). By arguments quite similar to those from
S_{\neq}
, these terms are negligible and therefore absolutely summable, and we omit further details. Thus we need only consider the cases when
\varphi_{2^{m}}
and
\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}
have supports which intersect the set of frequencies whose modulus takes values in
[\lambda_{q}\Gamma_{q}^{-1},\lambda_{q}\Gamma_{q}]
. In such case, we claim that there exists
q_{1}
such that for all
q\geq q_{1}
, (
2.31
) is satisfied by any kernel
\|\check{\varphi}\|_{1}^{-2}\check{\varphi}_{2^{m}}\ast\check{\varphi}_{2^{m^{\prime}}}
. Indeed the first condition in (
2.31
) follows obviously from the Young’s convolution inequality. For the second condition, we choose a ball
B_{R}
which contains the support of
\widetilde{\varphi}
. This implies that
\mathrm{supp\,}(\varphi_{2^{m}})\subset B_{2^{m}R}
. Since
\mathrm{supp\,}(\varphi_{2^{m}})
intersects the set of frequencies whose modulus takes values in
[\lambda_{q}\Gamma_{q}^{-1},\lambda_{q}\Gamma_{q}]
, we have
2^{m}R>\lambda_{q}\Gamma_{q}^{-1}
. Similarly,
2^{m^{\prime}}R>\lambda_{q}\Gamma_{q}^{-1}
. Then, by the Schwartz decay of the base kernel
\varphi=\widetilde{\varphi}(k)-\widetilde{\varphi}(2k)
,
for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
C(p,\widetilde{\varphi})
such that

|\check{\varphi}(x)|\leq\frac{C(p,\widetilde{\varphi})}{1+|x|^{p}}\qquad\implies\left|2^{3m}\check{\varphi}(2^{m}x)\right|\leq\frac{2^{3m}C(p,\widetilde{\varphi})}{1+|2^{m}x|^{p}}\,.

Using that
2^{m},2^{m^{\prime}}>\lambda_{q}\Gamma_{q}^{-1}R^{-1}
, we have that for
p
sufficiently large and
q\geq q_{0}
, where
q_{0}
depends on
p
and thus
\widetilde{\varphi}
,

\displaystyle\|\check{\varphi}_{2^{m}}\ast\check{\varphi}_{2^{m^{\prime}}}\|_{L^{1}(|x|\geq\lambda_{q}^{-1}\Gamma_{q}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|x|\geq\lambda_{q}^{-1}\Gamma_{q}^{2}}\left[\int_{|y|\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{q}^{-1}\Gamma_{q}^{2}}+\int_{|y|\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{q}^{-1}\Gamma_{q}^{2}}\right]2^{3(m+m^{\prime})}|\check{\varphi}(2^{m}y)||\check{\varphi}(2^{m^{\prime}}(x-y))|dydx

\displaystyle\leq\|\check{\varphi}\|_{1}\int_{|y|\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{q}^{-1}\Gamma_{q}^{2}}2^{3m}|\check{\varphi}(2^{m}y)|dy

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{|y|\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{q}^{-1}\Gamma_{q}^{2}}2^{3(m+m^{\prime})}|\check{\varphi}(2^{m}y)|\int_{|x-y|\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{q}^{-1}\Gamma_{q}^{2}}|\check{\varphi}(2^{m^{\prime}}(x-y))|dxdy

\displaystyle\lesssim_{C(p,\widetilde{\varphi}),R}\Gamma_{q}^{-p+3}\leq\|\check{\varphi}\|_{1}^{2}\lambda_{q}^{-1000}\,.

Therefore,

\|\check{\varphi}\|_{1}^{-2}\check{\varphi}_{2^{m}}\ast\check{\varphi}_{2^{m^{\prime}}}
satisfies
(
2.31
), and (
2.32
) holds for
\widehat{w}_{q}
.
We have reduced the finiteness of
S_{=}
to the finiteness of

\displaystyle\sum_{\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}m,m^{\prime},q\,:\\
|m-m^{\prime}|\leq M,\\
\mathrm{supp\,}(\varphi_{2^{m}},\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}})\cap[\lambda_{q}\Gamma_{q}^{-1},\lambda_{q}\Gamma_{q}]\neq\emptyset,\\
\eqref{summy}\textnormal{ holds}\end{subarray}\right\}}\left|H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}}}\mathbb{P}_{\varphi_{2^{m}}}(\widehat{w}_{q}),\widehat{w}_{q}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\check{\varphi}\right\|_{1}^{2}\sum_{q}\sum_{\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}m,m^{\prime}\,:\\
\mathrm{supp\,}(\varphi_{2^{m}},\varphi_{2^{m^{\prime}}})\cap[\lambda_{q}\Gamma_{q}^{-1},\lambda_{q}\Gamma_{q}]\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}\right\}}\Gamma_{q-{\bar{n}}-4}^{-1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\check{\varphi}\right\|_{1}^{2}\sum_{q}\Gamma_{q-{\bar{n}}-4}^{-1}\log_{2}(\lambda_{q}\Gamma_{q})\lesssim\left\|\check{\varphi}\right\|_{1}^{2}\sum_{q}\Gamma_{q-{\bar{n}}-4}^{-1}b^{q}\log_{2}(a)<\infty\,,

where we have used (
2.5a
). This concludes the proof of (
1.8
).

Step 3: Checking (
1.7
).

Let
\varphi
be a Schwarz function with unit mean. We start with the case that
\phi:=\widehat{\varphi}\in\mathcal{A}
, and aim to check that for any given
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
, there exists
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}(\bar{\varepsilon})
such that

\left|h(t)-H\left(\varphi_{\epsilon}\ast u,u\right)\right|=\left|h(t)-H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}}u,u\right)\right|<\bar{\varepsilon}\,.

(2.43)

holds true for any
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
, where
\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}:=\phi(\epsilon\cdot)
.
Let
q_{\epsilon}
be the smallest integer such that
\lambda_{q_{\epsilon}+{\bar{n}}}\Gamma_{q_{\epsilon}+{\bar{n}}}^{-1}>|k|
for all
k
in the support of
\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}
. We decompose the helicity above into

\displaystyle H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}}u,u\right)

\displaystyle=H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}}u_{q_{\bar{m}}+1},u_{q_{\bar{m}}+1}\right)+H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}}(u-u_{q_{\bar{m}}+1}),u_{q_{\bar{m}}+1}\right)+H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}}u,u-u_{q_{\bar{m}}+1}\right)

\displaystyle=:H_{1}(t)+H_{2}(t)+H_{3}(t)\,.

Since none of the frequencies in the support of
\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}
are in the support of the active frequencies of
u-u_{q_{\bar{m}}+1}
, by repeating arguments similar to
Step 2
, we can choose
\epsilon_{0}
small enough so that
|H_{2}(t)|+|H_{3}(t)|<\nicefrac{{\bar{\varepsilon}}}{{5}}
. Moving to
H_{1}(t)
, we split

\displaystyle H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}}u_{q_{\bar{m}}+1},u_{q_{\bar{m}}+1}\right)

\displaystyle=H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}}u_{q_{\bar{m}}-1},u_{q_{\bar{m}}-1}\right)+2H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}}(u_{q_{\bar{m}}-1}),\widehat{w}_{q_{\bar{m}}+{\bar{n}}}+\widehat{w}_{q_{\bar{m}}+{\bar{n}}-1}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}}(\widehat{w}_{q_{\bar{m}}+{\bar{n}}}+\widehat{w}_{q_{\bar{m}}+{\bar{n}}-1}),\widehat{w}_{q_{\bar{m}}+{\bar{n}}}+\widehat{w}_{q_{\bar{m}}+{\bar{n}}-1}\right)=:H_{11}(t)+H_{12}(t)+H_{13}(t)\,.

Using arguments similar to those in
Step 2
, which are predicated on the disjoint active frequency support of separate velocity increments, we can choose
\epsilon_{0}
sufficiently small so that
|H_{12}(t)|<\nicefrac{{\bar{\varepsilon}}}{{5}}
. In addition, exploiting Hypothesis
2.7
as in
Step 2
,
we can choose
\epsilon_{0}
small enough so that
|H_{13}|<\nicefrac{{\bar{\varepsilon}}}{{5}}
.
Next, by choosing
\epsilon_{0}
sufficiently small based on
\varphi
, we can ensure that
\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}
takes value
1
when

|k|\leq\lambda_{q_{\epsilon}+{\bar{n}}-2}\Gamma_{q_{\epsilon}+{\bar{n}}-2}
, which is possible due to the superexponential growth of frequencies and
\phi\in\mathcal{A}
.
Therefore
\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}
satisfies the assumption for the multiplier required in (
2.29
), and so

\displaystyle\left|h(t)-H\left(\mathbb{P}_{\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}}(u_{q_{\epsilon}-1}),u_{q_{\epsilon}-1}\right)\right|\leq\Gamma_{q_{\epsilon}-2}^{-1}<\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}}{5}

if
\epsilon
is sufficiently large.
Combining all the estimates, we have (
2.43
).

We now outline the steps needed to prove (
1.7
) for general Schwarz functions
\varphi
with unit mean. Noting that
\phi:=\widehat{\varphi}
satisfies
\phi(0)=1
,
let
\{\phi^{\delta}\}
for
\delta>0
be a family of multipliers such that the following hold. First,
\phi^{\delta}(z)\equiv\phi(z)
for all
2\delta\leq|z|\leq\delta^{-1}
,
\phi^{\delta}(z)\equiv 1
for all
|z|\leq\delta
, and
\phi^{\delta}(z)=0
for all
|z|\geq 2\delta^{-1}
. Next, we construct
\phi^{\delta}
so that there exists
C
such that for all
\delta\in(0,1)
,
3
3

3

\phi^{\delta}
can be constructed by adjusting
\phi
so that it is constant in a ball of radius
\approx\delta
around the origin, then mollifying only near the origin with a mollifier at scale
\approx\delta
.

\|\nabla^{M}(\phi-\phi^{\delta})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{2\delta}(0))}\leq C\delta^{1-M}\,,\qquad M=0,1,2,3,4\,.

(2.44)

Then
\phi^{\delta}\in\mathcal{A}
for
\delta\in(0,1)
, and it is enough to prove that there exists a small
\delta=\delta(\bar{\varepsilon},\varphi)>0
such that for any small
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\left|H(\varphi_{\varepsilon}\ast u,u)-H(\mathbb{P}_{\phi^{\delta}_{\epsilon^{-1}}}u,u)\right|=\left|H(\mathbb{P}_{\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}-\phi^{\delta}_{\epsilon^{-1}}}u,u)\right|<\bar{\varepsilon}.

(2.45)

To this end, observe that
\|\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}-\phi^{\delta}_{\epsilon^{-1}}\|_{\infty}
and
\|\check{\phi}_{\epsilon}-\check{\phi}^{\delta}_{\epsilon}\|_{1}
are of order
\delta
, since

\displaystyle\|\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}-\phi^{\delta}_{\epsilon^{-1}}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\check{\phi}_{\epsilon}-\check{\phi}^{\delta}_{\epsilon}\|_{1}=\|\check{\phi}-\check{\phi^{\delta}}\|_{1}\leq\|\check{\phi}-\check{\phi^{\delta}}\|_{L^{1}(B_{\delta^{-1}})}+\|\check{\phi}-\check{\phi^{\delta}}\|_{L^{1}(B_{\delta^{-1}}^{c})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{-3}\|\check{\phi}-\check{\phi^{\delta}}\|_{\infty}+\delta\||x|^{4}(\check{\phi}-\check{\phi^{\delta}})\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\delta^{-3}\|\phi-\phi^{\delta}\|_{1}+\delta\|\Delta^{2}(\phi-\phi^{\delta})\|_{1}\lesssim_{\|\widehat{\varphi}\|_{\mathcal{S}}}\delta,

where the last line follows from the Schwartz decay of
\phi
and (
2.44
). Using this and the linearity of (
2.32
)–(
2.33
) in the kernel
\varphi
, repeating earlier arguments gives the bound
|H(\mathbb{P}_{\phi_{\epsilon^{-1}}-\phi^{\delta}_{\epsilon^{-1}}}u,u)|\lesssim_{\|\widehat{\varphi}\|_{\mathcal{S}}}\delta
, leading to (
2.45
). ∎

3
Helical building blocks

3.1 Mikado bundles and other preliminaries

We start with a series of preliminary facts from
[
29
,
6
]
, with some slight adjustments related to the helicity.

Proposition 4.1,
[
29
]

(
Geometric lemma
)
.

Let
\Xi={\Xi_{0}}=\left\{\nicefrac{{3}}{{5}}e_{i}\pm\nicefrac{{4}}{{5}}e_{j}\right\}_{1\leq i<j\leq 3}
. Let
{\bar{n}}\in\mathbb{N}
be a given natural number. Then there exists
\epsilon>0
and disjoint sets
\Xi_{0},\Xi_{1},\dots,\Xi_{{\bar{n}}-1}\subset\mathbb{Q}^{3}\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}
such that every symmetric 2-tensor in
B(\mathrm{Id},\epsilon)
can be written as a unique, positive linear combination of
\xi_{j}\otimes\xi_{j}
for
\xi_{j}\in\Xi_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq{\bar{n}}
.
i.e., there exists
\gamma_{\xi_{j}}\in C^{\infty}(B(\mathrm{Id},\epsilon);(0,\infty))
such that

\displaystyle R=\sum_{{\xi_{j}\in\Xi_{j}}}\left(\gamma_{{\xi_{j}}}(R)\right)^{2}{\xi_{j}\otimes\xi_{j}},\quad\forall R\in B(\mathrm{Id},\epsilon)\,.

Definition 3.1

(
Vector directions
)
.

The sets
\Xi_{0},\dots,\Xi_{{\bar{n}}-1}
are obtained by applying rational rotations to the fixed set
\Xi
and will be used to produce simple tensors which cancel Reynolds stresses; we choose the rational rotation matrices such that none of
\Xi_{0},\dots,\Xi_{{\bar{n}}-1}
contain
\vec{e}_{2}
. We also require
{\bar{n}}
-many vector directions
\vec{h}_{0},\dots,\vec{h}_{{\bar{n}}-1}
used to correct the helicity profile. We set
{\vec{h}_{0}}=\vec{e}_{2}
and then apply rational rotation matrices to obtain
\vec{h}_{2},\dots\vec{h}_{n}
, ensuring that for all
1\leq j_{1}\neq j_{2}\leq{\bar{n}}
,

\left(\Xi_{j_{1}}\cup\{\vec{h}_{j_{1}}\}\right)\cap\left(\Xi_{j_{2}}\cup\{\vec{h}_{j_{2}}\}\right)=\emptyset\,,

(3.1)

so that vector directions used at a given step of the iteration are always distinct from the vector directions used in the previous
{\bar{n}}-1
steps of the iteration.

The next results are nearly verbatim from
[
29
]
, the only adjustment being the incorporation of the vector directions needed to correct the helicity.

Proposition 4.4,
[
29
]

(
Rotating, Shifting, Periodizing
)
.

Fix
\xi\in{\bigcup_{j=0}^{{\bar{n}}-1}}\left(\Xi_{j}\cup\{\vec{h}_{j}\}\right)
from Proposition
4.1, []
and Definition
3.1
. For any such
\xi
, we choose
\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}^{3}\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}
such that
\{\xi,\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We then denote by
n_{\ast}
the least positive integer such that
n_{\ast}\xi,n_{\ast}\xi^{\prime}n_{\ast}\xi^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
for all
\xi\in{\bigcup_{j=0}^{{\bar{n}}-1}}\left(\Xi_{j}\cup\{\vec{h}_{j}\}\right)
. Let
{r^{-1},\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}
be given such that
\lambda r\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\varkappa:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function with support contained inside a ball of radius
\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
. Then for
k\in\{0,...,r^{-1}-1\}^{2}
, there exist functions
\varkappa^{k}_{\lambda,r,\xi}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined in terms of
\varkappa
, satisfying the properties listed in
[
29
, Proposition 4.4]
.

We now recall the basic intermittent pipe flows used in
[
6
,
29
]
.

Proposition 4.5,
[
29
]

(
Intermittent pipe flows for Reynolds stress).

Fix a vector direction

\xi\in{\bigcup_{j=0}^{{\bar{n}}-1}}\left(\Xi_{j}\cup\{\vec{h}_{j}\}\right)
from Proposition
4.1, []
and Definition
3.1
,
r^{-1},\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
with
\lambda r\in\mathbb{N}
, and large integers
\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}
and
{\mathsf{D}}
. There exist vector fields
\mathcal{W}^{k}_{\xi,\lambda,r}:\mathbb{T}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
for
k\in\{0,...,r^{-1}-1\}^{2}
and implicit constants depending on
\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}
and
{\mathsf{D}}
but not on
\lambda
or
r
such that:

(1)

There exists
\varrho:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
given by the iterated divergence
\mathrm{div}^{\mathsf{D}}\vartheta=:\varrho
of a pairwise symmetric tensor potential
\vartheta:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with compact support in a ball of radius
\frac{1}{4}
such that the following holds. Let
\varrho_{\xi,\lambda,r}^{k}
and
\vartheta_{\xi,\lambda,r}^{k}
be defined as in Proposition
4.4, []
, in terms of
\varrho
and
\vartheta
(instead of
\varkappa
). Then there exists
\mathcal{U}^{k}_{\xi,\lambda,r}:\mathbb{T}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that
if
\{\xi,\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime}\}\subset\mathbb{Q}^{3}\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}
form an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
\xi\times\xi^{\prime}=\xi^{\prime\prime}
, then we have

\mathcal{U}_{\xi,\lambda,r}^{k}=-\frac{1}{3}\xi^{\prime}\underbrace{\lambda^{-{\mathsf{D}}}\xi^{\prime\prime}\cdot\nabla\left(\mathrm{div}^{{\mathsf{D}}-2}\left(\vartheta_{\xi,\lambda,r}^{k}\right)\right)^{ii}}_{=:\varphi_{\xi,\lambda,r}^{\prime\prime k}}+\frac{1}{3}\xi^{\prime\prime}\underbrace{\lambda^{-{\mathsf{D}}}\xi^{\prime}\cdot\nabla\left(\mathrm{div}^{{\mathsf{D}}-2}\left(\vartheta_{\xi,\lambda,r}^{k}\right)\right)^{ii}}_{=:\varphi_{\xi,\lambda,r}^{\prime k}}\,,

(3.2)

and thus

\displaystyle\mathrm{curl\,}\mathcal{U}^{k}_{\xi,\lambda,r}=\xi\lambda^{-{\mathsf{D}}}\mathrm{div}^{\mathsf{D}}\left(\vartheta^{k}_{\xi,\lambda,r}\right)

\displaystyle=\xi\varrho^{k}_{\xi,\lambda,r}=:\mathcal{W}^{k}_{\xi,\lambda,r}\,,\qquad\xi\cdot\nabla\vartheta_{\xi,\lambda,r}=0\,.

(3.3)

(2)

\{\mathcal{U}_{\xi,\lambda,r}^{k}\}_{k}
,
\{\varrho_{\xi,\lambda,r}^{k}\}_{k}
,
\{\vartheta_{\xi,\lambda,r}^{k}\}_{k}
, and
\{\mathcal{W}_{\xi,\lambda,r}^{k}\}_{k}
satisfy the conclusions from
[
29
, Proposition 4.4]
.

(3)

\mathcal{W}^{k}_{\xi,\lambda,r}
is a stationary, pressureless solution to the Euler equations.

(4)

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\mathcal{W}^{k}_{\xi,\lambda,r}\otimes\mathcal{W}^{k}_{\xi,\lambda,r}=\xi\otimes\xi}
.

(5)

For all
n\leq 3\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}
,

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{n}\vartheta^{k}_{\xi,\lambda,r}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{n}r^{\left(\frac{2}{p}-1\right)},\qquad{\left\|\nabla^{n}\varrho^{k}_{\xi,\lambda,r}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}\lesssim\lambda^{n}r^{\left(\frac{2}{p}-1\right)}}

(3.4)

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{n}\mathcal{U}^{k}_{\xi,\lambda,r}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{n-1}r^{\left(\frac{2}{p}-1\right)},\qquad{\left\|\nabla^{n}\mathcal{W}^{k}_{\xi,\lambda,r}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}\lesssim\lambda^{n}r^{\left(\frac{2}{p}-1\right)}}.

(3.5)

(6)

We have that
\mathrm{supp\,}\vartheta_{\xi,\lambda,r}^{k}\subseteq B\left(\mathrm{supp\,}\varrho_{\xi,\lambda,r},2\lambda^{-1}\right)
.

(7)

Let
\Phi:\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{T}^{3}
be the periodic solution to the transport equation
\partial_{t}\Phi+v\cdot\nabla\Phi=0
with initial datum
\Phi|_{t=t_{0}}=x
, with a smooth, divergence-free, periodic velocity field
v
. Then
\nabla\Phi^{-1}\cdot\left(\mathcal{W}^{k}_{\xi,\lambda,r}\circ\Phi\right)=\mathrm{curl\,}\left(\nabla\Phi^{T}\cdot\left(\mathcal{U}^{k}_{\xi,\lambda,r}\circ\Phi\right)\right)
.

(8)

For any convolution kernel
K
,
\Phi
as above,
A=(\nabla\Phi)^{-1}
, and for
i=1,2,3
,

\displaystyle\bigg[\nabla\cdot\bigg(A\,K\ast\left(\mathcal{W}^{k}_{\xi,\lambda,r}\otimes\mathcal{W}^{k}_{\xi,\lambda,r}\right)(\Phi)A^{T}\bigg)\bigg]_{i}

\displaystyle=A_{m}^{j}\xi^{m}\xi^{l}\partial_{j}A_{l}^{i}\,K\ast\left(\left(\varrho^{k}_{\xi,\lambda,r}\right)^{2}(\Phi)\right)\,.

(3.6)

We now recall the bundling pipe flows. Since only quadratic cancellations are used in this paper, some simplifications have been made relative to
[
29
, Proposition 4.9]
.

Proposition 4.9,
[
29
]

(
“Bundling” pipe flows
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\xi}^{k}
)
.

Fix a vector direction
\xi\in{\bigcup_{j=0}^{{\bar{n}}-1}}\left(\Xi_{j}\cup\{\vec{h}_{j}\}\right)
. Then for
k\in\{1,\dots,\Gamma_{q}^{6}\}
, there exist bundling pipe flows
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\xi}^{k}
satisfying the following.

(1)

\boldsymbol{\rho}^{k}_{\xi}
is
\left(\nicefrac{{\mathbb{T}}}{{\lambda_{q+1}\Gamma_{q}^{-4}}}\right)^{3}
-periodic and satisfies
\xi\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{\rho}^{k}_{\xi}\equiv 0
.

(2)

\{\boldsymbol{\rho}^{k}_{\xi}\}_{k}
satisfies the conclusions of Proposition
4.4, []
with
r^{-1}=\Gamma_{q}^{3}
,
\lambda=\lambda_{q+1}\Gamma_{q}^{-1}
. In particular,
\mathrm{supp\,}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{k}_{\xi}\cap\mathrm{supp\,}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{k^{\prime}}_{\xi}=\emptyset
for
k\neq k^{\prime}
, and there are
\Gamma_{q}^{6}
disjoint choices of placement.

(3)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\xi,k}^{2}=1
.

(4)

For all
n\leq 3\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}
and
p\in[1,\infty]
,

\left\|\nabla^{n}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{k}_{\xi}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}\lesssim\left(\Gamma_{q}^{-1}\lambda_{q+1}\right)^{n}\Gamma_{q}^{-3\left(\frac{2}{p}-1\right)}
.

Now we recall the anistropic cutoffs, on which we will chose placements for different bundles.

Definition 4.10,
[
29
]

(
Strongly anisotropic cutoffs
)
.

To each
\xi\in{\bigcup_{j=0}^{{\bar{n}}-1}}\left(\Xi_{j}\cup\{\vec{h}_{j}\}\right)
, we associate a partition of the orthogonal space
\xi^{\perp}\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
into a grid
4
4

4

We refer to the grid used in Proposition
4.4, []
, as any periodicity issues have been avoided there.
of squares of sidelength
\approx\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{{\bar{n}}}}{{2}}}^{-1}
. We index the squares
\mathcal{S}
in this partition by
I
. To this grid, we associate a partition of unity
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\xi}^{I}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\xi}^{I}=\begin{cases}1&\textnormal{ on }\frac{3}{4}\mathcal{S}_{I}\\
0&\textnormal{ outside }\frac{5}{4}\mathcal{S}_{I}\end{cases},\qquad\sum_{I}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\xi}^{I})^{2}=1\,,

(3.7)

which in addition satisfies
(\xi\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\xi}=0
and
\left\|\nabla^{N}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\xi}^{I}\right\|_{\infty}\lesssim\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{{\bar{n}}}}{{2}}}^{N}
for all
N\leq 3\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}
and all
I
. We can bound the cardinality of the index set
I
by
|\{I\in\mathcal{S}\}|\lesssim\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{{\bar{n}}}}{{2}}}^{2}
.

We now recall the
intermittent pipe bundles
from
[
29
, Definition 4.12]
. We do not define these for the vectors
\vec{h}_{j}
, as those vectors will be used to define distinct “helical pipe bundles” later.

Definition 3.2

(
Intermittent pipe bundles
)
.

For each
\xi\in{\bigcup_{j=0}^{{\bar{n}}-1}}\Xi_{j}\subset\mathbb{Q}^{3}\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}
, we define intermittent pipe bundles by
\mathbb{B}_{\xi}=\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\xi}\sum_{I}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\xi}^{I}\mathbb{W}_{\xi}^{I}=\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\xi}\sum_{I}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\xi}^{I}\xi\varrho_{\xi}^{I}
, where
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\xi}=\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\xi}^{m}
is defined as in Proposition
4.9, []
for some
m=m_{\xi}
,
\mathbb{W}_{\xi}^{I}:=\mathcal{W}^{k}_{\xi,\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}},\nicefrac{{\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{{\bar{n}}}}{{2}}}\Gamma_{q}}}{{\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}}}}}
, and
\varrho_{\xi}^{I}:=\varrho^{k}_{\xi,\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}},\nicefrac{{\lambda_{q+\nicefrac{{{\bar{n}}}}{{2}}}\Gamma_{q}}}{{\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}}}}}
is defined as in Propositions
4.5, []
for some
k
.

3.2 Helical pipe bundles

We now define the helical pipe bundles.

Proposition 3.3

(
Helical pipe bundles
)
.

Let
\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin},{\mathsf{D}}\in\mathbb{N}
be large integers. Define

\displaystyle\Psi_{q}^{\pm}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})

\displaystyle=(x_{1}\mp\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}}^{-1}\sin(\lambda_{q*}x_{2}),x_{2},x_{3}+\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}}^{-1}\cos(\lambda_{q*}x_{2}))^{\top}

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{\pm\vec{e}_{2},\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}},r_{q}}^{k}

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{e}_{2}^{\pm}\,\varrho_{\vec{e}_{2},\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}},r_{q}}^{k}(\Psi_{q}^{\pm})\,,\qquad\qquad\widetilde{e}_{2}^{\pm}=(\nabla\Psi_{q}^{\pm})^{-1}\vec{e}_{2}

(3.8)

where
\varrho^{k}_{\vec{e}_{2},\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}},r_{q}}
is defined in Proposition
4.5, []
. We define helical pipe bundles by

\mathbb{B}_{\vec{e}_{2},\pm}=\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\vec{e}_{2}}\sum_{I}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\vec{e}_{2}}^{I}\mathbb{H}^{I}_{{\pm}\vec{e}_{2},\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}},r_{q}}\,.

(3.9)

The
I
indicates that the placement (i.e. the value of
k
) depends on the cutoff
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\vec{e}_{2}}^{I}
. Then there exist implicit constants depending on
\mathsf{N}_{\rm fin}
and
{\mathsf{D}}
but not on
q
such that the following hold.

(1)

\varrho=\varrho_{\vec{e}_{2},\lambda_{q+{\bar{n}}},r_{q}}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is given by the iterated divergence
\mathrm{div}^{\mathsf{D}}\vartheta=:\varrho
of a pairwise symmetric tensor potential
